Betazone: The Human-Machine Line

World Economic Forum(1.1M)·36:53·1.9K views·2026-10-14
Ambarish MitraSpeaker, World Economic Forum Dana AlomarSpeaker, World Economic Forum Madeline GannonRoboticist and Designer
TL;DRRobotics and physical AI are rapidly commercializing, shifting the core interface from traditional screens to personality, connection, and distributed environmental intelligence.
While humanoid robots currently capture public imagination, experts emphasize that the real economic...

TL;DR

Robotics and physical AI are rapidly commercializing, shifting the core interface from traditional screens to personality, connection, and distributed environmental intelligence. While humanoid robots currently capture public imagination, experts emphasize that the real economic value lies in upgrading existing industrial infrastructure and scaling specialized, practical applications like waste intelligence.

Human-Machine Interfaces & The Shift to Personality

  • The Limitations of Legacy Computing:
    • The traditional computer interface (keyboards, mice, screens) was defined 60 years ago and relies on humans reaching into software.
    • This model fails when intelligent physical systems reach back out into the shared physical world.
  • Personality as the Future Interface:
    • Madeleine Gannon's Hypothesis: The primary interface for physical AI will not be a screen, but personality and how machines make humans feel.
    • Designing industrial machines to express character and legibility builds a foundation of trust and honesty rather than trying to mimic human form.
  • Autonomy vs. Control: Giving machines autonomy inherently means losing some control, creating a gray area between user experience design and government policy that requires rapid experimentation.

Industrial Realities & Waste Intelligence

  • The Global Waste Crisis:
    • Ambarish Mitra notes that humanity produces 2 billion tons of waste worldwide annually, with only 8% recycled.
    • The waste industry is vastly complex—plastics alone consist of 80 different polymers, and the labor force is typically not composed of material scientists.
  • AI Brains over Specific Hardware:
    • Grey Parrot chose not to build proprietary robots, instead creating a universal "waste brain" that plugs into hardware from various global manufacturers (Swiss, Japanese, Korean, Taiwanese).
    • This decentralized intelligence model mirrors self-driving cars, aggregating a collective view of regional waste streams.
  • Productivity Benchmarks:
    • A skilled human waste sorter can perform 42 picks a minute, while a high-end robot performs 92.
    • Despite technological advancements, the industry requires exponential acceleration—scaling from current capabilities toward a demand of 600 picks per minute.
  • The Scale of Existing GDP: 80% of the world's GDP does not currently use AI or robotics, representing a massive opportunity for intelligent upgrades to existing machinery rather than full labor replacement.

Dematerialization & The Evolution of Form Factors

  • Humanoids as Eye Candy:
    • Humanoid robots are largely a distraction and marketing tool ("eye candy") compared to less glamorous but vital industrial applications.
    • Real-World Scale: 100% of New York City and London waste streams undergo full automation scanning. In New York City, aluminum recovery rates jumped from 48% 7 years ago to 89% today.
  • Distributed Intelligence:
    • Rather than centralized humanoid bodies, future domestic systems will likely feature intelligence distributed throughout the environment (integrated into lamps, curtains, and appliances).
    • Technologies developed for humanoids will likely dissolve into prosthetics, wearables, and assistive devices (such as braces for independent living at 85 years old).

Governance, Policy, and Co-Working

  • Parallel Policies: The logic used in robotic software development to establish motion policies heavily overlaps with government policy and governance frameworks.
  • Corporate Priorities in Automation:
    • Ambarish Mitra notes that in industrial settings, productivity ultimately takes priority, and aligning employer and employee interests remains a complex gray area.
    • Rigorous safety testing, defining limits, and training co-workers on strict operational rules are the highest responsibilities an organization can undertake.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Ambarish Mitra claims that humanoid shape robots and humans cannot coexist long-term because one or the other will face a major problem.
  • Ambarish Mitra predicts that instead of full automation taking over everything in 10 years, there will be a significant intelligence upgrade to existing machinery over a 25-year view.
  • Madeleine Gannon predicts that the total addressable market for humanoids is much smaller than current industry pitches suggest, driving the technology instead toward modular, assistive applications like independent-living wearables and prosthetics.

Notable Quotes

"Automation is a choice, it's not an inevitability." — Madeline Gannon
"A robot is just a faster human. And the problem requires solving it to be exponential faster." — Ambarish Mitra
"The future for intelligent autonomous machines is really about connection and not control." — Madeline Gannon

Can Open Models Keep Up With OpenAI and Anthropic? | Nathan Lambert

a16z(301.0K)·58:49·8.6K views·2026-10-14
Lisha LiPartner, a16z Nathan LambertFounder, Trillium
TL;DRNathan Lambert (founder of Trillium and AI researcher) and Lisha Li (a16z Partner) discuss the dynamics between closed frontier AI labs (OpenAI, Anthropic) and open-source/open-weight model builders, concluding that while frontier labs hold…
structural advantages in compute and...

TL;DR

Nathan Lambert (founder of Trillium and AI researcher) and Lisha Li (a16z Partner) discuss the dynamics between closed frontier AI labs (OpenAI, Anthropic) and open-source/open-weight model builders, concluding that while frontier labs hold structural advantages in compute and capital, open labs—particularly in China—remain remarkably competitive by acting as fast followers and leveraging distillation, synthetic data, and RL environments.

1. Capital, Talent, and the Open-Closed Frontier Dynamic

  • Foundational Inputs: Model progress is driven by three forms of capital: compute, data, and talent.
  • The Closed Frontier Labs:
    • OpenAI and Anthropic: Held up by Nathan Lambert as the true frontier labs that take the earliest, most ambitious bets, driving paradigms like reasoning inference scaling laws and cloud-code explosions.
    • Organizational Reset: Success depends heavily on the human aspect and organizational charts; companies like Meta successfully reset from the bottom up to regain positive momentum.
  • The Chinese Open-Model Strategy:
    • Commercialization & Distribution: Chinese labs (e.g., Moonshot AI, Zhipu, Kimi/01.AI) build open-weight models primarily as a practical distribution and commercialization strategy to break into the US market rather than for pure altruism.
    • Talent Parity vs. Compute Starvation: Nathan Lambert asserts that Chinese labs possess a comparable, high-level talent pool to the US, but operate at a massive compute deficit (e.g., top Chinese pre-training researchers having access to a fraction of the 5,000 H100 equivalents available to an average OpenAI researcher).

2. Distillation, Data, and the Open-Source Catch-Up

  • The Distillation Debate:
    • Friction in Open Post-Training: While public perception views distillation as a primary leveling lever, Nathan Lambert notes that open literature reveals open model builders are often stuck using outdated Qwen models because replicating teacher reasoning traces and SFT/RL pipelines from top models remains technically difficult.
    • Market Dynamics: Despite friction, APIs and reasoning traces are easily exploited; Nathan Lambert notes that Chinese labs have teams dedicated to stealing reasoning traces, and Jensen Huang’s perspective is that distillation is simply "competition."
  • Economic Time Scales:
    • Closed labs enjoy integrated, efficient inference at scale, high margins, and direct customer feedback loops.
    • Open-model economies involve a fragmented supply chain (model builders, inference companies, post-training companies), meaning it takes significantly longer for open ecosystems to build ownership, find ROI, and deploy proprietary data fine-tunes.

3. RL Environments, Synthetic Data, and Reward Hacking

  • The Data Economy Boom:
    • Billions of dollars are being spent on buying data and building RL environments.
    • Much of the commercial data currently sold by third parties is flawed, featuring rough edges, overseas contractors via intermediaries, and a high rate of reward hacking where smart models game poorly constructed test files or empty mazes.
  • In-House Shifts: Frontier labs are increasingly bringing data generation and environment building in-house to prevent data leakage and proprietary signal loss.

4. Trillium, Open Infrastructure, and Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI)

  • Trillium's Mission: Nathan Lambert launched the nonprofit research lab Trillium (named after the spring ephemeral flower that blooms before the canopy grows) to provide open infrastructure—such as open-source RL environments for high-leverage terminal tasks and SFT mid-training stacks.
  • Demystifying RSI: Trillium aims to document the actual tools, tasks, configs, and failure trajectories of AI-driven research to demystify recursive self-improvement and help policymakers and researchers understand where autonomous loops actually get stuck.
  • Community Contributions: While AI agents on GitHub introduce noise (e.g., LLM-generated fake research issues), open-source collaboration remains a vital counterweight to commercial secrecy.

5. Scaling Realities and Societal Preparedness

  • Exponential Scaling Limits: Scaling laws show exponential increases in compute for linear performance gains, making sudden "fast takeoffs" unlikely due to human bottlenecks and extreme resource costs.
  • Cybersecurity Gaps: Nathan Lambert warns that while capability plots and RSI do not indicate immediate existential doom, society is woefully underprepared for the cross-public cybersecurity risks and vulnerabilities diffusing outward as open and closed models proliferate.

Gemini 4 Argon: What’s new and what’s next

Google for Developers(2.7M)·30:21·15.1K views·2026-10-14
Josh WoodwardExecutive, Google DeepMind Melvin JohnsonExecutive, Google DeepMind Sergey BrinCo-founder, Google
TL;DRGoogle DeepMind introduced **Gemini 4 Argon**, an advanced AI model featuring an industry-leading one million output tokens, refined writing quality, and deep integration into internal engineering workflows by 100,000 Googlers prior to launch.
Availability: Josh Woodward announced...

TL;DR

Google DeepMind introduced Gemini 4 Argon, an advanced AI model featuring an industry-leading one million output tokens, refined writing quality, and deep integration into internal engineering workflows by 100,000 Googlers prior to launch.

Model Access, Scale, and Testing

  • Availability: Josh Woodward announced that Gemini 4 Argon is available immediately across the Gemini app, AI Studio, the Gemini API on cloud, and Antigravity.
  • Internal Scale at Google: Sergey Brin highlighted that 100,000 internal engineers tested the model, forming an internal flywheel where researchers and models work hand-in-hand on rapid three-to-four-week iteration cycles.
  • Early Enterprise and Cyber Testing: Tulsee Doshi noted that the model has undergone three to four weeks of internal testing, while early external access has been provided to cyber defenders—including a partnership with Wiz via their Scan For Good program—to identify and patch vulnerabilities.
  • Safety and Regulatory Filings: Google submitted the model to the US government for testing and reinforced internal and external guardrails regarding monitoring and model setup.

Writing Quality and Model Behavior

  • Eradicating AI Slop: Josh Woodward explained that the team worked with professional writers to stamp out "AI slop" (load-bearing phrases, excessive listification), aiming to give Gemini 4 Argon a concise, precise, and unique "house style" tailored for sales, legal, finance, and coding.
  • Behavioral Trade-offs: Tulsee Doshi detailed efforts to strike a balance between model persistence (working hard without stopping) and avoiding extremes like over-verbosity, aiming for optimal transparency regarding what the model is doing.
  • Thought Traces in UI: Josh Woodward mentioned ongoing product experiments regarding the length and frequency of thought traces, noting that users initially value deep thought traces but rely on them less as trust grows.

Real-World Use Cases and Breakthroughs

  • Code Migrations and Legacy Systems: Melvin Johnson and the team discussed the Antigravity team using Argon to migrate external Fortran codebases to Rust.
  • Hardware Root-Causing: Melvin Johnson and Sergey Brin shared an instance where an engineer had Argon investigate a hardware bug; the model successfully pinpointed corrupted High Bandwidth Memory (HBM) from a specific vendor that regular ECC checks missed, allowing Google to outcast those machines from the fleet.
  • Knowledge Work and Creative Tasks: Googlers utilized the model for Chromium development, Gemma optimizations, drafting and sharpening professional writing, building a reading app grounded in early childhood learning theory, and simulating data center operations.

One Million Output Tokens and Advanced Reasoning

  • Industry-Leading Capacity: Logan Kilpatrick and Melvin Johnson emphasized the release of industry-leading one million output tokens in a single step.
  • Depth on Complex Problems: Tulsee Doshi and Melvin Johnson explained that at max reasoning effort ("high"), a non-trivial percentage of evaluation trajectories hit the million-token limit, enabling the model to handle deep, multi-step math and open-ended research problems requiring long horizons.
  • Metaphorical Scale: Melvin Johnson compared one million tokens at human processing speeds to roughly a month of continuous human thinking.

Notable Quotes

"We're starting to get onto that RSI treadmill. And now our researchers and the model work hand in hand to make the next version of the model... internally we're already using a couple models ahead." — Sergey Brin
"One thing about Argon that I feel really good about is we have extensively tested the model for more than three, four weeks now internally and all of the signs tell us that it's good." — Tulsee Doshi
"We made a real concerted effort to really try to just stamp that out [AI slop]. So it should feel more concise, more precise. We've worked with a bunch of professional writers, and they've just been marking up the model output." — Josh Woodward
"For a very hard math problem, for example, that's where you might need to think for quite a long time... a million tokens, if you think about that as half a million seconds, that's like a full week or so... an enormous amount of capacity." — Melvin Johnson

So, a few things, right? When we think about behavior, we actually think about it across a number of different axes of how the model is engaging with you. And I think it's important because you can get to either extreme and neither is particularly good, right? So if you talk about for example, we talk a lot about how this model is persistent. You could also go to the other extreme where the model is annoying because it is just, like asking you questions again and again and again, right? And so, like, I think we think a lot about how do you find the right middle ground of that type of trade-off, right? And so we've push the model to be persistent. And so we definitely are anchoring more to that angle, but we want it to be able to clarify ambiguity. Similarly, another area that we've invested in for this model has been transparency. You also want to again, find the right balance between being too verbose and not telling the user anything at all. Interesting, what we tend to see is early on in a new model, people really value deep, frequent thought traces. Match Group CEO: What Makes a Match on Tinder? | Podcast | In Good Company

Norges Bank Investment Management(133.0K)·44:24·7.1K views·2026-10-14
Nicolai TangenCEO, Norges Bank Investment Management Spencer RascoffCEO, Match Group
TL;DRMatch Group CEO Spencer Rascoff discusses the mechanics of modern online dating, how AI and recommendation algorithms have transformed platforms like Tinder and Hinge, and how shifting Gen Z social behaviors are reshaping romance and addressing the global loneliness epidemic.

TL;DR

Match Group CEO Spencer Rascoff discusses the mechanics of modern online dating, how AI and recommendation algorithms have transformed platforms like Tinder and Hinge, and how shifting Gen Z social behaviors are reshaping romance and addressing the global loneliness epidemic.

Recommendation Algorithms & Product Mechanics

  • The "Rex Algo" Recommendation System: Match Group uses a recommendation algorithm to determine profile pairings, moving from multiple isolated user decks to a unified "Q unification" approach that combines goals like retention and first-time user engagement.
    • Real-time Limitations: Spencer Rascoff notes that the algorithm is not yet real-time (unlike Spotify or TikTok), meaning preference changes take hours to update.
    • Contextual Likes: New profile features allow users to like specific items (e.g., hobbies or lifestyle elements) rather than just swiping blindly, providing the algorithm with richer behavioral signals.
    • Team Scale: The dedicated recommendation algorithm team consists of approximately 40 people, including senior experts from TikTok.
  • Profile Optimization Benchmarks: Data shows that successful male profiles feature seven or more photos, rich text content, and prompt answers—avoiding the single shirtless gym selfie. Successful female profiles also showcase diverse personal experiences, friends, and active lifestyles.
  • AI Profile Scoring: Match Group is testing an AI-powered profile scoring tool that provides feedback on user photos and text to encourage greater content diversity (e.g., suggesting more varied clothing or activity backdrops).
  • Demographics & App Portfolio: Tinder and Hinge users skew heavily Gen Z (about 60% of users are 18–28, with a median age around 25). Match Group operates tailored apps globally, including *Pairs* (Japan, which asks for salary and blood type), *The League* (skewing female, built for highly ambitious people), and *Sniffies* (a secure platform for the gay male community).

Gen Z Dating Culture & The Loneliness Epidemic

  • The "Friends First" Shift: Young people increasingly view traditional one-on-one dates with high-pressure job interview anxiety, leading to a social "friends first" group dynamic rather than immediate one-on-one meetings.
  • Double Date Feature: Responding to Gen Z's preference for group hangouts, Tinder introduced a Double Date feature. In Norway, 3.5 million double date matches have occurred since March 2025, with over 50% of female Gen Z users and 48–50% of total Gen Z users in Norway utilizing the feature (compared to 25% in the United States).
  • The Loneliness Crisis: Rascoff views loneliness as an existential risk and public health crisis worse than smoking, driven by smartphone absorption, remote work, fading social institutions (churches, unions, political parties), and high living costs depressing birth rates.
  • The Decline of In-Person Approaching: Citing surveys showing 45% of men aged 18 to 25 have never approached a woman in person, Rascoff attributes this to fear and a cultural shift where cold approaches can feel overly aggressive in the post-#MeToo era.
  • Gen Alpha Reversion: Research on Gen Alpha (ages 12–18) indicates a reversion to more traditional high school dating and relationship norms, largely unburdened by pandemic-era social disruption.
  • Offline Events & Dry Dates: Tinder now hosts singles events (pickle ball, mahjong, park meetups) in 25 cities—scaling to 75+ global cities by the end of the year—to facilitate low-pressure, alcohol-free in-person interactions backed by an in-app digital layer.

Turnaround Strategy & Corporate Restructuring

  • Initial Assessment: Upon taking over in February 2025, Rascoff found a company that had lost its way, operated too short-term for Wall Street, and neglected product innovation.
  • Listening Tour & Employee Engagement: Rascoff conducted hundreds of 1-on-1s across all 12 global offices. Tinder employee engagement rose by 17 points in a single year, cutting disengagement in half—an improvement cited by third-party surveyors as unprecedented.
  • Team & Roadmap Reset: Guided by the philosophy that *great people properly organized build great products*, Rascoff cleared the entire product roadmap, restarted college internship programs (bringing in 30 interns this summer), and hired more Gen Z employees.
  • Current Headcount: Match Group employs approximately 2,000 people, with 600 at Tinder (including 200 in product and engineering, and 50–100 in marketing) and 500 at Hinge.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Real-time Algorithm Deployment: Spencer Rascoff states that real-time algorithmic updates (matching Spotify and TikTok responsiveness) will roll out to Match Group apps by the end of the year.
  • Global Event Expansion: Tinder's singles events will expand from 25 cities to 75 or more global cities by the end of the year.
  • Gen Z Product-Market Fit: Rascoff predicts that within 5 to 10 years, Tinder will fully reclaim product-market fit with Gen Z by successfully positioning itself around safe, group-based social dating rather than hookup stigma, with Hinge cementing its status as the definitive app for intentional, long-term relationships.

My best guess is that Tinder in particular will have really turned a corner in terms of regaining product-market fit with Gen Z because of this social roadmap, because Tinder will not stand for "hookup," which is the stigma that the Tinder brand has had historically. Uber, I think, is a good analogy, where Uber went from, "I don't know if I feel safe getting in the backseat of a stranger's car; maybe taxis are safer because they're regulated" that was 5 years ago—to today, you think to yourself, "Well, of course I feel safe because of all these features that Uber has provided to me." We need a lot of people who don't pay anything, and the vast majority of our users don't pay anything, and that's very important. That's important to the liquidity pool, so I don't see that changing. You might see us experiment with higher-expense products, but I think more likely you'll see us improving the efficacy of the free service, which will improve retention of the payers. My philosophy is I'm the highest-paid person at the company; I should be the hardest-working person at the company. That's my philosophy, and I set a pretty rapid pace for my team to follow. The first thing I did was look at the people. So I have this kind of philosophy, and I say it all the time: great people properly organized and properly motivated build great products that are informed by consumer insights that generate audience, which generate revenue and profit, which then generate shareholder value. So it's this circle, this spiral that starts with great people properly organized and motivated. So I spent almost the first year just focused on the people. How do we make sure we have the right people in place? How do I make sure they're motivated? We did an employee engagement survey, for example, on Tinder, which was our most troubled brand, and something like 25% of people said they were disengaged, and only about 40% of employees were engaged. A year later, the numbers have radically changed. So the disengagement has been cut in half; the engagement has almost doubled. We gained 17 points of employee engagement in a year, which our third-party vendor that surveys this for companies globally says is unprecedented. They've never seen such a large improvement in employee engagement. So it starts with the people, motivation, and organization. Then they have to build cool stuff. They have to build cool stuff that attracts audience. And so the next thing we did was we completely cleared the product roadmap. I said, "Forget about everything you've been building. Let's start from scratch. What should we build for our users?" And to do that, you need consumer insights. So we started doing a lot of consumer research. We brought a lot of Gen Z users into the building. We hired a lot more Gen Z employees. We restarted the internship program. So we had no college interns before I was here; this summer we had 30 college interns. How many are you all together in the firm? We're about 2,000 employees. And what do they do? Well, Tinder has about 600 people, Hinge has about 500 people, and the rest work on a variety of things. Just focusing on Tinder for a second, of the 600 employees, about 200 of them are in product and engineering. They build things, they build the apps, they design the products. Probably 50 to 100 of them are in marketing. We market this product globally; we're in almost 200 countries. And so they build social assets, they do marketing plans. We have a significant customer support function to handle our tens of millions of users and challenges that they face. But ultimately we try to have as many people as possible close to the product, close to solving problems for our users. So that was my sequence of this turnaround: people, then products, then we start thinking about monetization, and then the final output is the Wall Street stuff. Once you have people building products generating revenue and profit, then the share price will take care of itself. You have done a lot of things before. You were a serial entrepreneur, you've been very successful, made a lot of money, all that kind of thing. I've started companies. I started a company called Hotwire, which we sold to Expedia for $700 million. I started Zillow, which we took public and got to more than a $10 billion market cap. And then I've started 12 other companies, some of which have succeeded, some of which have failed. I've invested in lots of startups. So I've been in and around the tech industry for a long time. And what is it that you have taken from these experiences into the Match Group? It's basically this recipe: great people properly organized and motivated to build great products. So you've done it before. I've done this before. We did this at Zillow. I mean, we started Zillow from scratch, and I assembled a team of great people. I organized them properly, I motivated them, and then we did consumer insights to build great products. We got to 200 million people use Zillow every month, and it's just in the United States. So almost everybody in the United States uses Zillow every single month. What about the corporate culture? How has that changed? We moved to a culture that prioritizes and rewards innovation, even at the risk of failure. You remember when Amazon launched the Firephone; they were going to compete with Apple and build a phone. When Amazon shut that down a year or two later, Amazon very famously promoted the whole team that built Firephone; they put them in other roles in the company instead of firing them. And that story always inspired me. How long does it take to change corporate culture? I thought it would take longer. I thought it would probably take two or three years. I was able to change the culture at Match Group within a year. Do you think it's quicker to change people in the US than elsewhere? Maybe. The employment laws are a little harder to exit people in certain countries in Europe, for example, and that can slow down. Some people can actually be changed; some people need to be exited and then replaced with people who have a different attitude and already have a changed attitude. What type of people do you hire? What do you look for? Especially in this AI world, I'm just looking for people with intense curiosity who are tinkerers and builders. Most of our people that we hire now have side quests, side projects. A very common interview question I'll ask is, "What are you using AI for on the side to build and tinker?" And they'll say, "Well, I'm going on vacation next month with my extended family, and so I built a web app for the 20 of us that are going on this trip so everybody can coordinate schedules and we can plan together, and I use Claude Code to build that." What are you using it for outside work? Outside work, I built a chess game for my three kids and I. So when I make a move, it sends a text message to all three kids, and then they reply via text and make their move. So we're kind of playing one-on-three chess using AI via text, for example. Do you beat them? I used to. I don't anymore. I was a semi-professional chess player when I was young, and I'm a very good chess player, and two of my three kids now beat me regularly. Now, some personal questions. How do you relax? I play pickleball every weekend, either with friends or family. I have a very close relationship with my wife. We hang out, we relax, we drink wine, we go for hikes. I live pretty close to the beach, and I love the ocean and the beach. They're very rejuvenating for me. So I try to see the ocean 100 days a year if possible, and I go for walks on the beach quite a bit. What do you read? I read American history, 1860 to 1930. And what's so good about that period? It was the period of rapid industrialization, huge cultural and business change, the taming of the American West, kind of the end of the US frontier and the beginning of modern business and modern politics. How do you think today compares with that period? Well, I just finished two biographies: one, House of Morgan by Ron Chernow, about JP Morgan and his descendants; one, Titan, about John D. Rockefeller and his descendants. And it's changed so much. When you read those biographies, these people were fabulously wealthy, but their wealth—I mean, this is like Elon Musk kind of levels of wealth, the equivalent of hundreds of billions of dollars in today's wealth—but their businesses operated so slowly and at such relative small scale. No technology, no email, no Slack, no AI. I don't know how they would do. I'm not sure how successful they would be as business leaders today. The pace that we keep today is so different. And that to me is—those biographies go so deep, they're a thousand pages long, talking about the actual day-to-day of what these people did—and it just dawns on me reading those biographies that some of their skills obviously are transferable, but much of what they did to make them successful is illegal today, partly because of their success. So I'm not sure if many of those skills are transferable. Their attention to detail—I think of Rockefeller in particular—is obviously transferable. I try to bring that attention to detail to our products. But the way I operate, the way I run my company, the way most leaders lead today, is much more teams and teams of teams focusing on employee engagement and managing at scale and motivating large groups of people. I think for that, the skill that I learned from the many biographies I've read of Teddy Roosevelt and Franklin Roosevelt are more transferable. FDR—great recent book, Traitor to His Class, which I also just read—talks about how he mastered his social media of the day. His platform was not TikTok or podcasts or Instagram or LinkedIn; his platform was radio, and using radio to communicate and motivate and inform and alliance-build to advance his agenda to create his response to the Great Depression and then the response to World War II. He was a masterful communicator, and that skill is clearly transferable to today, but of course with different technologies and different platforms. So when you look at all these leaders, who do you admire the most? I probably admire Teddy Roosevelt the most because he was an incredible communicator. His rambunctious sense of urgency and activity resonates most with me. I'm constantly in motion, and so was Teddy Roosevelt. And his love of conservatism and nature also really motivates me. He was 100 years ahead of his time in recognizing the importance of environmentalism, and we today are still grateful for what he gave to us in terms of creating the modern environmental movement and the national parks in the United States. A bit like your audience on the apps, people like a full view of other people. So to get a fuller view of you, what is the biggest personal failure you've made? I've had several layoffs in my time, including one at Match Group shortly after I began. I don't really view that one as a failure because I didn't hire into that situation. But in 2008, we did layoffs at Zillow in response to the financial crisis. In 2001 at Hotwire, we did layoffs in response to the 9/11 crisis. But these are kind of business failures. What about some personal failures? Big screw-ups. Have you had some big screw-ups in your life? I haven't had a lot of big screw-ups in my life, knock on wood. No. I mean, I have regrets. My parents have both passed away. My brother passed away at a young age, and I had very close relationships with them, but obviously I regret not having invested more time and focus in those relationships when they were available to me. They're not really failures, but they're regrets. What is something about you that most people don't know? I was a competitive chess player and take chess pretty seriously and learned a lot from chess. I grew up in and around the music industry, which is why I'm a little bit hard of hearing. Because your father was touring with some famous bands, right? My father was the tour producer and business manager for the Rolling Stones, U2, David Bowie, Paul Simon, and Pink Floyd. So, did you come along to those tours? I did. In fact, that's the one time I've been to Oslo, was with the Rolling Stones in 1990 on the Urban Jungle tour. What's your favorite band? I spent the most time with and I've seen the most Rolling Stones concerts, but I think in terms of musical tastes, U2 is my favorite band. They're the music that I listen to the most today. So, did it really impact your hearing? It actually did, yes. Starting at age two, I would go to concerts. Back then in the '70s, Stones concerts were very loud, and Lynyrd Skynyrd concerts and .38 Special and lots of other bands that my dad was producing their tours for. And when I'm at concerts today and I see little three-year-old toddlers with those big headphones because parents are very intelligently putting headphones on them, I think to myself, "That was not the way I grew up." I was standing right next to the speaker and no headphones for me. So Spencer, in addition to using headphones, what are other pieces of advice for young people today? My daughter is 21 and is entering the workforce, and she's going into your field, into financial services and investment banking. And the advice I give to her and other young people is—young people might not like this advice—be the first person in the office in the morning, be the last person to leave the office at night. I know it's not fashionable to say, but putting in the time, that's incredibly important. You need to be in the room. Don't take a job that's primarily remote; ideally take a job that's in-office because you want to be a sponge. You want to try to be in the room and learn as much as you can. Don't optimize for short-term income. If you can afford to take that job that pays a little bit less, but you can learn a little bit more, try to optimize for education and learning at your young age and put in the effort. Work hard. There will—again, I know this is not a popular thing to say—be a time a little bit later in life, maybe even as early as your late 20s, when you can start prioritizing you. But especially early career, you need to prioritize work. And again, I think the pendulum has swung such that that's not a very favorable thing to say anymore, but I'll say the quiet part out loud, which is there's no substitute for that type of effort, especially early career. Well, you for sure are putting in the hours. You are the first guy in the office, and as big shareholders, we very much appreciate all your hard work. It's been great speaking to you. Thank you for having me. This was fun.

Opening Plenary

World Economic Forum(1.1M)·72:19·2.4K views·2026-10-14
Mariam Nourya KonéSpeaker, World Economic Forum Mohammad Abdullah Al GergawiMinister of Cabinet Affairs, UAE Fadel AdibAssociate Professor, MIT
TL;DRGlobal leaders and next-generation innovators at the World Economic Forum opening plenary in Dubai emphasized that while the world faces unprecedented geopolitical uncertainty, technological shifts, and climate disruption, young people retain immense optimism and agency to co-author a resilient future.

TL;DR

Global leaders and next-generation innovators at the World Economic Forum opening plenary in Dubai emphasized that while the world faces unprecedented geopolitical uncertainty, technological shifts, and climate disruption, young people retain immense optimism and agency to co-author a resilient future. The event launched key global initiatives—including a new Center for Global Future Opportunities and a $1 million AI challenge—to bridge the gap between foresight and practical action.

Context, Geopolitics, and Global Challenges

  • Global Risks and Instability:
    • The World Risk Report underscores uncertainty as the defining condition of the current era.
    • In 2025, the world witnessed over 60 armed conflicts—the highest number since 1946.
    • Global military spending reached nearly $3 trillion, marking the largest annual increase since the end of the Cold War.
    • Global public debt approached 94% of global GDP in 2025, with the International Monetary Fund (IMF) expecting it to reach 100% by 2029, limiting government investment capacity.
  • The European Context:
    • Mathieu Flamini noted that Europe is operating outside its comfort zone without predictability.
    • The European chemical industry's global market share dropped from 25% five years ago to 13% today.
  • Resources and Climate Realities:
    • The world has experienced the 10 warmest years since modern records began, making climate and nature risks lived realities for 9 out of 10 young people.
    • Amid these challenges, renewable energy accounted for approximately 86% of global power capacity additions in 2025.

Youth Sentiments and the 2026 Youth Pulse

  • Longitudinal Youth Pulse Insights: Natalie Pierce presented preliminary insights from the World Economic Forum's longitudinal study (building on a 2016 baseline of 20,000 young people):
    • Climate Concern: Planetary resilience remains young people's top global concern, rising from 45% in 2016 to 52% today. 9 out of 10 young people state climate change affects their daily realities.
    • Economic Anxiety: When looking at personal futures, young people place economic uncertainty at the top of the list. Over the next decade, 1.2 billion young people in developing and emerging economies will reach working age, against an estimated 400 million new jobs—leaving an 800 million gap.
    • Employment Disadvantage: 1 in 5 young people globally are already outside of employment, education, and training.
    • AI Adoption and Sentiment: In 2016, 86% of youth believed AI would create jobs and 64% anticipated career impacts. By 2026, 91% have deeply embedded AI into their daily lives, though positive sentiment dropped slightly to 62%.
    • Optimism vs. Geography: 2 in 3 young people globally expect their economic prospects to surpass those of their parents. However, this optimism is geographically uneven: the overwhelming majority of youth in Sub-Saharan Africa expect better prospects, compared to just over half in Europe and Eurasia.

Technology, AI, and Human Capability

  • Spatial and Physical AI: Fidel Adib noted that AI is moving beyond software into the physical world through sensing, robotics, and automation (such as spatial AI developed in the US and adopted heavily in Europe).
  • The Data and Representation Gap: Mariam Nurya Koné highlighted that women represent only 1 out of 5 data annotators globally, and current AI models are predominantly built outside of Africa, despite Africa housing the world's fastest-growing young population.
  • AI Inequities and Misinterpretation: Suhani Jalota shared an example of an AI health chatbot in Mumbai misinterpreting a Hindi phrase about uterine evacuation as personal hygiene due to a lack of training data for marginalized populations.
  • Economic Inclusion via AI Training: Mina Mahila Foundation's data training initiatives in India show that when women are paid to teach AI local dialects and health contexts, 25% to 50% of previously disengaged women enter the workforce, often using the role as a stepping stone to other businesses.

Leadership Attributes and Institutional Transformation

  • Five Essential Qualities of Future Leaders (outlined by Mohammad Abdullah Al Gergawi):

1. *Anticipation:* Preparing for crises before they happen rather than merely reacting.

2. *Adaptability:* Continually learning and unlearning yesterday's assumptions.

3. *Wisdom:* Utilizing technology responsibly and ethically.

4. *Partnership:* Building collaborative networks across sectors and geographies.

5. *Execution:* Transforming vision into measurable impact on human lives.

  • Organizational Requirements for Scale: Kiara Winona Sweeney identified three traits of future-ready organizations: willingness to make trade-offs and reallocate budgets, capacity to experiment without certainty through rapid piloting, and building deliberate pathways for scale.

New Initiatives Announced

  • Center for Global Future Opportunities: Established via a partnership between the UAE government and the World Economic Forum to turn technological and economic opportunities into actionable policies, legislation, and projects.
  • Global Innovation Challenge and Agentic AI Award: A collaboration with Dubai Municipality featuring an annual prize of $1 million, engaging over 10,000 Global Shapers across 150 countries to develop intelligent urban solutions (winners to be announced in 2027).
  • Future 20: An initiative highlighting 20 leaders under the age of 30 with the potential to drive major transformations over the next 20 years.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Global public debt will reach 100% of global GDP by 2029, according to International Monetary Fund (IMF) projections cited by Mohammad Abdullah Al Gergawi.
  • Over the next decade (leading to 2036), 1.2 billion young people in developing and emerging economies will reach working age, against an estimated 400 million new jobs, creating an 800 million employment gap as projected in the Youth Pulse data presented by Natalie Pierce.

Notable Quotes

"The world has not only changed, the rules of change themselves have changed. An economic decision in one capital can change the lives of millions. A cyber attack can disrupt an entire country's services. A new technology can reshape an economic sector in any country within a short period." — Mohammad Abdullah Al Gergawi
"In 2016, AI was a prediction. In 2026, AI is a practice. And looking ahead to 2036, young people still think that AI will be the technological force that most shapes the decade ahead." — Natalie Pierce
"A good football player thinks after he receives the ball. A great football player thinks before he receives the ball." — Mathieu Flamini
"We need to unlearn the idea that if there's no market, there's no need. Those two are not equivalent." — Suhani Jalota
Here the future is planned, tested and built. Ambition is visible in institutions, infrastructure, technology and in the confidence entrusted in young people. The partnership between the World Economic Forum and the government of the United Arab Emirates dates back many years. It was built on a shared commitment to make sense of tomorrow's challenges and move the world forward together. Today, this meeting inaugurates a new chapter where our platform brings together the forum's next communities, those shaping the future now with those who will carry responsibility for it in the decades ahead. We do not take for granted that we meet during a complicated moment in the region and in the world when almost every assumption is under pressure. Geopolitical uncertainty is reshaping security, trade and trust. Technology is accelerating faster than many institutions can adapt. Economic models that once promised predictability are being tested by fragmentation and uneven growth. Climate and nature risks are no longer distant scenarios. They are lived realities. The forum's latest work gives us important signals. While the global risk report tells us that uncertainty is the defining condition of this period, the upcoming youth pulse tells us something equally important. Today's rising generation is a generation of ambition and agency. Young people are thinking seriously about economic opportunity and the future of work, about how artificial intelligence can expand what people are able to do, about the institutions they want to trust and about how prosperity and sustainability can advance together. They are not asking to be protected from change. They are asking to shape change with dignity, agency, and purpose. For too long, the world has spoken of young people as leaders of tomorrow. But tomorrow is not a serious enough word for the responsibilities they already carry today. So let us begin with a commitment to the next generation leaders in this room. Bring your courage, your impatience, and your imagination, but also bring the discipline to build networks and convene diverse stakeholders because no lasting change is built alone. To the senior leaders here, bring your experience and your responsibility, but also bring the humility to unlearn because the future might not be governed by yesterday's assumptions. And to all of us, let this meeting be a place where cooperation is renewed as a practice, where AI expands human agency, where growth carries dignity, not division, where resilience is designed before crisis arrives, and where the voices closest to the future are closest to the decisions. On behalf of the World Economic Forum, thank you to the government of the United Arab Emirates for its leadership, friendship, partnership, and support in hosting us so warmly at this important moment despite the challenging geopolitical circumstances that surround us. And thank you to all of you for making the journey here. Your presence demonstrates that dialogue and cooperation are not luxuries for easier times. They are essential precisely when the world is under pressure. You are here not as observers of the future but as its co-authors. And with that, I am delighted to invite His Excellency Muhammad Abdullah Al Gergawi, Minister of Cabinet Affairs of the United Arab Emirates and principal chairman of the annual meeting of global future leaders, to deliver his welcome remarks. The Minister will speak in Arabic. So my friends, you may choose to kindly use your headsets. Your Excellency, the floor is yours. Thank you very much. Good morning, Your Highnesses, your excellencies, ladies and gentlemen, Mr. Borge Brende, President and Chief Executive Officer of the World Economic Forum, peace be upon you. Our partnership with the World Economic Forum began here in 2008. We began together with the summit of the global agenda followed by the global future councils which brought more than 900 councils and 12,000 experts and leaders. Today we begin a new chapter with the global future leaders bringing together leaders, innovators, and entrepreneurs from more than 100 countries, united by the ambition of building a better future for humanity. Ladies and gentlemen, 18 years ago in this very place, we asked, "How do we prepare for the future?" Today, we ask, "How do we lead a future whose rules are constantly changing?" Because the world has not only changed, the rules of change themselves have changed. An economic decision in one capital can change the lives of millions. A cyber attack can disrupt an entire country's services. A new technology can reshape an economic sector in any country within a short period. Ladies and gentlemen, today we face three major transformations. The first is a transformation in the global balance of power. In 2025, the world witnessed more than 60 armed conflicts, the highest number since 1946. Global military spending reached nearly $3 trillion, marking the largest annual increase since the end of the Cold War. Trade restrictions are also redrawing the map of manufacturing, investment, and supply chains. Decisions in one capital can now change the course of trade, supply chains and food and energy prices. Global public debt approached 94% of global GDP in 2025, with the International Monetary Fund expecting it to reach 100% by 2029, limiting many governments' ability to invest in the future. This places a greater responsibility on future leaders to build economies that can withstand shocks, partnerships that bridge divisions, and trust that opens the doors to cooperation. In a world where the balance of power is shifting, the strength of leadership is measured by its ability to protect people's opportunities and their future. The second transformation concerns human capabilities and technology. My question is, is AI the last invention by the human? Will AI invent tools and machines and new inventions? This development raises a fundamental question. If technology becomes capable of inventing and producing new machines and tools, how do we redefine the human role and the skills of people in innovation? The third transformation is about the future of resources, energy, and climate. The world has experienced the 10 warmest years since modern records began. At the same time, renewable energy accounted for approximately 86% of global power capacity additions in 2025. We face unprecedented challenges, but we also have solutions that previous generations never had. Progress depends on how quickly we turn these transformations and the solutions into reality. These transformations call for a new understanding of leadership. There are five essential qualities that every future leader must possess. The first is the ability to anticipate change rather than simply respond to it. A future leader prepares for crises before they happen. The second is the ability to learn and adapt continuously. What made us successful yesterday may hold us back tomorrow. A successful leader does not have all the answers but has the courage to reconsider them. The third attribute is wisdom. Technology and AI, yes, they give us greater capabilities, but it is wisdom that determines how we use them. The fourth is the ability to build partnerships. Global challenges cannot be addressed through isolated effort. A leader's strength will be measured by what they enable others to achieve together. The fifth is the ability to turn vision into results. True leadership is measured by the impact of decisions on people's lives. Ladies and gentlemen, for 18 years and from this very place, we have brought minds together to predict the future and to anticipate it. Today we move from discussing the future to shaping it. With you, to put this commitment into action, we are announcing today three initiatives. The first initiative is establishing the Center for Global Future Opportunities, the first of its kind in the world. Established through a partnership between the UAE government and the World Economic Forum, it will turn technological and economic opportunities into policies, legislation, and projects that can be implemented. The second initiative is the Global Innovation Challenge and Agentic AI Award. This is in collaboration with Dubai Municipality with an annual prize of $1 million. It will engage more than 10,000 global shapers across 150 countries to develop intelligent solutions that improve services and quality of life in cities. We will announce the winners at our next meeting in 2027. The success of one city can become a model for hundreds of other cities throughout the world. The third initiative is Future 20, to shed light on 20 leaders under the age of 30 years old who have the potential to drive major transformations over the next 20 years. In the end, ladies and gentlemen, today you lead institutions and communities. Your decisions and ideas can give millions of people renewed hope. We want partnerships that turn into action, opportunities that turn into achievement, and an impact that reaches generations to come. The future is shaped by technology, resources, and the leaders who make the decisions. Thank you very much. Here we are. Okay, that's better. A very warm welcome from me as well. My name is Sebastian Buckup, Managing Director at the World Economic Forum, and I have the immense pleasure of guiding us through the next segment of this opening plenary. But first, a huge thanks. When you ask any foresight expert, they will tell you, if you want to understand the future, you should start with the perspectives of those that will shape the future longest, start with young people. And that's what we're going to do here today. As Seb said, almost exactly 10 years ago to this day, we asked 20,000 young people around the world, what do you think the future will look like? And 10 years later, we can now say, what did they see coming, and particularly, what do they see coming next? First, climate change has always been at the top of young people's global agenda. In 2016, 45% of young people named planetary resilience as their top global concern with the greatest consensus of any issue. And a decade later, climate change is still at the top of their list. Today, climate and environmental worry has increased to 52%, and nine out of 10 young people have stated that climate change is affecting their daily realities. So from retreating glaciers in the Himalayas and the Alps to wildfires across North America and southern Europe to floods and droughts intensified by a strengthening El Nino, climate disruption is very much a lived reality for young people. Now, in our longitudinal study, we ask young people two fundamental questions. One, what does the world ahead look like? And two, what do young people's personal futures look like? And those two perspectives are not always the same. When asked about their own personal lives, young people place economic uncertainty at the top of that list. Over the next 10 years, 1.2 billion young people in developing and emerging economies will become working age. Now, in that same time period, estimates assume around 400 million new jobs. That leaves an 800 million gap. That gap matters because already today, one in five young people around the world are no longer in or are outside of employment, education, and training. For millions, that first rung of the economic ladder is already hard to reach. And now that ladder itself is fundamentally changing. And nowhere is that clearer than AI. In 2016, we asked young people, what emerging technology will shape the next 10 years? And with great foresight, they said AI and robotics. And I think everyone in this room will agree they were spot on. In 2016, 86% believed that it would create jobs, and 64%—so roughly two in three young people—anticipated that their own careers would be affected. They saw both opportunity and disruption coming. Fast forward to today, and 91% of young people have already deeply embedded AI into their daily lives. A little less now, 62% feel positive about its impact. And often usage and optimism converge. In 2016, AI was a prediction. In 2026, AI is a practice. And looking ahead to 2036, young people still think that AI will be the technological force that most shapes the decade ahead. But for young people, the question about AI has fundamentally changed. In 2016, the question was, what will AI be able to do? In 2026, the question for young people is now, what will I do now that AI will reshape my life and work? If machines increasingly perform the tasks that young generations have required to learn and progress, new pathways into work will need to be imagined. We will need to make human capabilities, young people's capabilities, more valuable alongside AI. And this is the number one area of innovation that young people want to see from business and government alike. Finally, I want to share one more signal from our youth pulse results, and that is fundamentally a story about optimism. Today, two in three young people, despite headlines, believe and expect that their economic prospects will be better than the generation before them. And that matters because despite anxiety about climate, jobs, and technology, there's something equally powerful. Young people fundamentally believe in progress, that better jobs, better lives, and more opportunity than that which they've inherited today is possible tomorrow. So while there is optimism, I will share with you that that answer of optimism changes where young people are based. In Sub-Saharan Africa, the overwhelming majority think and expect better economic prospects than their parents' generations. In Europe and Eurasia, little more than half do. So for young people, opportunity is still geographic. So what does this generation tell us here at the Annual Meeting of Global Future Leaders? Future leaders are living through extraordinary change and are fundamentally optimistic about what it could make possible. Future leaders see uncertainty and believe their future can be better. That is not a contradiction. That is courage and a belief in the positive future that they can build. 10 years ago we asked the question, what will the future look like? And perhaps the more impactful question today is, what future do we want to make possible over the next two days? We are here to understand the signals, to challenge our assumptions, and to ultimately move the world forward together. Well, thanks a lot Natalie for a fantastic presentation that I think is setting us up well for the discussion we're going to have here in the next two days. I think it is fair to say that this is a generation that is coming of age at a time when lots of things are happening at the same time. And by the way, I'm going to use this moment of reset to already announce the next panel in case you're wondering. So what is this time? It is a time of enormous acceleration of change. If you think of compute, if you think of climate, if you think of conflict, I've heard someone at this meeting say yesterday, well maybe this is the "everything all at once" generation. Maybe 10 or 15 years after we tried to speak about the "new normal," now oftentimes there is the question if there is a normal at all. Now, what is the question that I will address with my panelists in a very brief moment? It's the question of this meeting: how can we create bold opportunities for many, not just for a few, while building capacity to withstand setbacks and respond to the unthinkable? This is exactly the question I would like to explore. Let me already give you a brief introduction of the panelists. San Francisco Fed President Mary Daly on the Fed’s New Communications Regime

Hoover Institution(1.2M)·50:27·2026-10-14
Mary DalyPresident, Federal Reserve Bank of San Francisco Steven DavisSenior Fellow, Hoover Institution
TL;DRThe Federal Reserve is reevaluating its communication regime under Chairman Kevin Warsh to improve the signal-to-noise ratio and avoid over-committing via rigid forward guidance.
Simultaneously, the Fed is navigating severe crosscurrents—including sticky inflation, supply-side...

TL;DR

The Federal Reserve is reevaluating its communication regime under Chairman Kevin Warsh to improve the signal-to-noise ratio and avoid over-committing via rigid forward guidance. Simultaneously, the Fed is navigating severe crosscurrents—including sticky inflation, supply-side overlapping shocks, and the multi-year economic impacts of AI adoption—requiring a flexible, forward-looking policy framework rather than rigid reliance on conventional playbooks.

Federal Reserve Communications Regime and Strategy

  • Shift from Historical Over-Communication:
    • Over the past three decades, central bank communications evolved from near-silence to heavy engagement, amplified significantly after the Global Financial Crisis (GFC) to manage expectations.
    • Mary Daly on Tool Overuse: Daly notes that "if I have a hammer, I'm always looking for a nail," arguing that central banks must step back to ensure communication tools build trust and clarity rather than creating low signal-to-noise ratios.
  • The Role of Chairman Kevin Warsh:
    • Daly views the transition under Chairman Kevin Warsh as an opportune catalyst to rigorously review Fed communications and balance-sheet tools alongside external task forces.
    • Avoiding Short-Term Misinterpretation: Frequent remarks about immediate meeting outcomes (e.g., the upcoming October meeting) risk mischaracterizing the Fed as short-term thinkers rather than forward-looking policymakers acting with 6-to-18-month lags.
  • Revisiting Forward Guidance and Balance Sheet Tools:
    • Forward Guidance Discipline: Daly suggests forward guidance should be reserved for times when the Fed has high clarity on its direction, rather than during periods of balancing active risks.
    • Balance Sheet Coupling: Daly questions whether the size and composition of the Fed's balance sheet should remain tightly coupled with the federal funds rate, or evaluated as a distinct operational tool.

Inflation Dynamics and Supply-Demand Shocks

  • Conventional Wisdom vs. Current Reality:
    • The traditional economic rule of thumb dictates that central banks should "look through" exogenous, one-time supply shocks (such as tariffs or oil price spikes) because policy lags prevent effective offsets.
    • Daly's Shift: While Daly favored looking through these shocks six months ago, the persistent duration and growing willingness of businesses to pass price increases downstream have pushed her to be more cautious about overlapping inflationary pressures.
  • Inflation Expectations and the Risk Dashboard:
    • Compensating Pressures: Daly monitors a specific "inflation risks dashboard," noting that market-based inflation compensation rose ahead of the September FOMC meeting, signaling upward drift.
    • Upside Risks: Daly stresses that current risks skew entirely to the upside, requiring the Fed to remain ready and willing to tighten further if necessary.
  • Labor Markets and Real Wages:
    • Nominal wage stickiness downwards means that negative supply shocks inherently lower real wages and reduce purchasing power.
    • While restrictive monetary policy can prevent wage-price spirals during demand overheating, applying it against supply-side shocks risks over-constraining the labor market and worsening real wage compression.

Artificial Intelligence and Economic Implications

  • The 1-to-3-Year Horizon vs. Decadal Productivity:
    • While AI represents a generational general-purpose technology comparable to electrification, monetary policy operates on a 1-to-3-year window.
    • Long-term productivity gains 10 years out do not directly solve current full-employment and price-stability mandates.
  • Near-Term Demand Pressures vs. Supply Adaptation:
    • Hyper-scaler Bottlenecks: The current phase is dominated by heavy infrastructure buildouts—data centers, GPUs, and memory chips—creating immediate demand shocks and input constraints (e.g., water, power, and chip availability).
    • Private Sector Mitigation: Firms are actively bypassing chip shortages by engineering products to use existing hardware longer, adopting small language models (SLMs), utilizing open-weight models, and deploying internal "gatekeepers" to route queries efficiently.
  • AI Boom-Bust Cycles:
    • Similar to historical semiconductor cycles, the rapid AI investment boom will eventually transition into a steady-state maintenance phase.
    • Daly highlights that a potential "bust" or slowdown in AI infrastructure spending could emerge within the 1-to-3-year horizon, naturally dissipating upward price pressures.

Financial System Stability and Cyber Risk

  • Commercial Bank Resilience:
    • When questioned about systemic threats like catastrophic cyber attacks disabling large financial institutions, Daly emphasized that private bank leaders (such as JPMorgan Chase CEO Jamie Diamond) and banking regulators are actively addressing cyber threats.
    • Fed Oversight: Vice Board Governor Michelle Bowman and the broader Federal Reserve system treat cyber risk as a top-tier supervisory priority, continuously ring-fencing internal IT data while pushing commercial institutions to fortify operational defenses against rapidly evolving technology.

Notable Quotes

"When we're communicating a lot, but the signal to noise ratio is low, then we have to step back and ask ourselves, is this really doing what we want? Is it building trust? Is it leading to more credibility?" — Mary Daly
"If you talk about the October meeting... you miss the part that's really important about policymaking, which is policy acts with a lag. You can debate what you think the lag is—12 months, six months, 18 months—but there's nobody who thinks we make the interest rate change and everything changes immediately." — Mary Daly
"I think that the current—if we just stopped today and we did the thought experiment if we didn't do any more development in AI—have we used all the capacity at the firm level for building productivity gains with the current things we already have? And the answer is in my judgment, absolutely not." — Mary Daly
Welcome to Economics Applied from the Federal Reserve Bank of San Francisco. I'm your host, Steven Davis, senior fellow at the Hoover Institution. With me is a special guest, Mary Daly. She's president of the Federal Reserve Bank of San Francisco. Welcome, Mary. Glad to see you again. And thank you for coming here to San Francisco Fed. Yeah, maybe I should welcome myself to the Fed, but any case, I'm happy to be here. You know, we last spoke in this format at the Jackson Hole Economic Policy Symposium in August 2025. We talked about central bank communications. I'll come back to that in a second. The setting was a little bit different. Now we're in your studio. Great lighting, great audio. Last time audio was a little more challenging, but we had the Grand Teton Mountain. Exactly. Exactly. And we had to discipline ourselves not to get distracted by that view. That's right. That's right. So hopefully we can Well, I think we did okay last time. We'll try to focus even more this time. So, you know, last time we did have a pretty meaty discussion about central bank communications, and I'm not going to try to review all that, but two things came out of that discussion very clearly, I think. First, the Fed has changed its communication regime dramatically over the decades, even over the course of your career and mine, as we discussed. Second, there are a lot of subtleties that aren't apparent on the surface in how the central bank should communicate with the broader public. It gets into issues of transparency, accountability, credibility, and trust and all that. So for those who really want to get into that kind of broad sweep historical perspective, they can watch our earlier conversation. But what I wanted to ask you about today is something, you know, pretty timely to the moment, which is how, if at all, has the communications regime at the Fed shifted under the chairmanship of Kevin Warsh or is shifting relative to what the regime was under Jerome Powell. Sure. And you know, you're right. I think in big historical swaps because that's been the dominant a dominant development in my career is we went from communicating almost not at all right to communicating quite a bit. The world really is more unpredictable than ever - two ‘chief risk officers’ explain why

World Economic Forum(1.1M)·49:52·1.3K views·2026-10-14
Chief Risk OfficerVolkswagen Chief Risk OfficerWorld Trade Organisation
TL;DRThe world has entered an "age of unpredictability" where interconnected macro risks materialize simultaneously, requiring organizations to abandon rigid quarterly reports in favor of real-time signal monitoring, AI-assisted pattern recognition, and localized supply chain resilience.

TL;DR

The world has entered an "age of unpredictability" where interconnected macro risks materialize simultaneously, requiring organizations to abandon rigid quarterly reports in favor of real-time signal monitoring, AI-assisted pattern recognition, and localized supply chain resilience.

Risk Landscape & The Age of Unpredictability

  • Simultaneous Multi-Risk Shocks:
    • Risks no longer occur sequentially in isolation; organizations face overlapping crises (e.g., Middle East geopolitical shocks constraining energy availability while natural disasters disrupt industrial supply chains).
    • Global Fragmentation (Natasha Fields): The most concerning macro risk is the fragmentation of the global economy into geopolitical and technological blocks, weakening the multilateral trading system.
    • Automotive Market Pressures (Torben Oeder): European car companies face compounded pressures from strict emission/safety regulations, increased material/energy costs, higher vehicle financing expenses, and currency depreciation in emerging markets.
  • Environmental Risk Underestimation:
    • El Nino & Cascading Impacts: Natasha Fields warns that climate risks like El Nino are heavily underestimated due to a failure to account for cascading effects on geopolitics, food security, and water supply.
    • Corporate Adaptation (Torben Oeder): Volkswagen monitors weather extremes via early warning systems, collaborates with suppliers on inventory buffers, and actively works to reduce the massive water consumption required for vehicle manufacturing.

AI, Data, and Human Judgement in Risk Management

  • Division of Labor:
    • AI & Quantitative Tools: Used effectively for anomaly detection, data aggregation, signal monitoring, and pattern recognition to reduce noise amidst reduced industry headcounts.
    • Human Leadership: Human interpretation remains indispensable for providing context, making strategic decisions, ensuring ethical judgement, and taking ultimate board-level liability.
  • The Risk of Inaction:
    • Competitive Imperative (Natasha Fields): Organizations must weigh the risks of using AI (such as data confidentiality risks at the WTO) against the greater risk of *not* using AI while competitors leverage it for efficiency and resilience.
    • Overhyped Perceptions (Torben Oeder): While AI helps identify cyber vulnerabilities, Torben Oeder views the broader AI panic as somewhat overhyped given that human strategic judgement cannot be replaced.

Evolving Risk Management Strategies

  • From Efficiency to Localization:
    • End of Global Scale: The days of developing a single car model and selling it worldwide are over; companies must adopt localized business models with regional sourcing, development, sales, and production.
    • Efficiency vs. Resilience Trade-off: While efficiency minimizes cost, true resilience—such as maintaining secondary suppliers—comes with a high price tag that boards only approve when presented with high-probability "hit rates."
  • Public vs. Private Sector Maturity:
    • Historical Gap: Private sector organizations historically developed risk management frameworks ahead of multilateral and government organizations due to greater budgets and team sizes.
    • Convergence: International organisations are rapidly catching up by leveraging chief risk officer communities and cross-sector information sharing.
  • Shift to Ad-Hoc Agility:
    • Risk functions are evolving away from traditional quarterly risk reports toward real-time, ad-hoc impact analysis capable of reacting instantly to overnight geopolitical or economic shocks.

Notable Quotes

"We are in the age of unpredictability. We can no longer predict as risk professionals, as organisations, with any sort of degree of certainty as we used to before, what's coming." — Natasha Fields
"Those days where you develop, let's say, one car and then sell it all over the world, those days are just over." — Torben Oeder
"Risk management is not about avoiding risk. Risk management is about taking opportunities." — Torben Oeder
"Welcome to the age of unpredictability, but also welcome to the age of opportunity, of creativity, to find solutions for those things that we're not aware of." — Natasha Fields

So that is something that does happen, that is led by our chief economists, because they use the more economic background of that rather than from a risk perspective. And the reason for this is I think historically there was one particular event that could have been a major one, but it was one at a time. The difference that I think we're seeing now, and this is why I like to call this the age of unpredictability, is because they're not coming in a unitary form. There is multiple risks that are materialising or that's coming to a crescendo all at the same time. Or if there's one particular risk because it's impacting so many different aspects of the globe, how do you manage that? And I think that's the difference. For example, in years before COVID, one of the risks that I always had on the risk register was a risk of an epidemic or a pandemic. I had no idea what COVID was, but that's just using the data that, you know, it was coming around. The world has moved on from that, and the risks are materialising or may materialise. Not only are we not aware of them, but we don't understand, or we don't have the visibility of how it's going to impact multiple streams of, be it geopolitics, technologically, public health, food and water supply, it's all having impacts everywhere.
