Thrive Capital’s Dr. Nworah Ayogu on Why Neuralink Is More Interesting Than SpaceX

Sourcery with Molly O'Shea(75.7K)·70:25·3.0K views·2026-10-14
Molly O'SheaHost, Sourcery Nworah AyoguPartner, Thrive Capital
TL;DRDespite the U.S.
healthcare system spending $40 trillion over the past decade with zero improvement in national life expectancy, an unprecedented convergence of AI, hardware, and biology is creating a major inflection point. Backed by venture firms like Thrive Capital, frontier...

TL;DR

Despite the U.S. healthcare system spending $40 trillion over the past decade with zero improvement in national life expectancy, an unprecedented convergence of AI, hardware, and biology is creating a major inflection point. Backed by venture firms like Thrive Capital, frontier companies are revolutionizing the value chain—from AI-driven drug discovery and brain-computer interfaces to continuous remote monitoring and decentralized consumer care—to dramatically improve patient outcomes and quality of life.

Healthcare Economics vs. Outcomes: The $40 Trillion Deficit

  • Stagnant Life Expectancy: Dr. Nworah Ayogu notes that the U.S. spent approximately $40 trillion on healthcare over the past 10 years with life expectancy remaining flat at roughly 80 years, representing a major indictment of the current system.
  • System Scale & Disparities: U.S. healthcare spending currently sits at $5 billion per year and is projected to reach $9 trillion by the end of the decade, making it the nation's largest employer (accounting for 20% of all jobs, with 30% of new jobs projected to be in healthcare) and the federal budget's largest line item at six times the size of defense.
  • The Tale of Two Cities: Life expectancy varies dramatically based on geography and income, citing a roughly 15-year gap between the richest 1% and poorest 1% of men.

AI and Biotech: Revolutionizing Drug Discovery and the Value Chain

  • Solving Biological Risk and Binding: AI platforms like Isomorphic Labs (founded by Demis Hassabis) tackle target identification (biological risk), off-target side effects, and protein folding/molecular design to tackle traditionally "undruggable" pockets.
  • The Journey from Mice to Humans: Research historically suffered from a paradox where cancer was "cured in mice 50 times" but failed in humans; AI and advanced data generation are accelerating the bridge from in vitro (cell) and in vivo (animal) testing to clinical success.
  • Scaling Ambition Like Eli Lilly: Lily achieved trillion-dollar status and surpassed competitors like Novo Nordisk by innovating across the entire value chain—mastering manufacturing scale and supply constraints a year ahead of rivals, while unblocking commercial access through direct-to-consumer partnerships (Lily Direct).
  • Personalized "End-of-One" Medicine: Breakthroughs like customized gene-editing treatments (e.g., treating rare metabolic disorders like baby KJ in months) and mRNA cancer vaccines paired with checkpoint inhibitors (such as Keytruda, which showed a 50% reduction in melanoma recurrence over 5 years) highlight the shift toward hyper-personalized care.

Hardware-Software Intersections: Neuralink and Foretell

  • Neuralink's Brain-Computer Interfaces: Dr. Ayogu argues that Neuralink is a more interesting company than SpaceX because the brain is vaster than space and serves as the fundamental substrate for all intelligence and compute. Operating as a bilingual organization of hardware builders, neurosurgeons, and even an in-sourced pathology lab, Neuralink uses microscopic threads and proprietary surgical robots to restore functionality (such as controlling computers, robotic arms, or speech synthesis) to paralyzed patients and those with ALS.
  • Foretell's Hearing Loss Innovations: Hearing loss affects 1.5 billion people and is the greatest modifiable risk factor for dementia (increasing risk by one-third). Foretell broke the industry's evaluation scale by scoring a 6 on a 5-point scale through custom silicon tape-outs at TSMC, semantic noise processing inspired by Zoom, and all-day battery life in a discreet form factor.

AI Agents and Care Delivery: OpenEvidence and Cadence

  • OpenEvidence Adoption: Two-thirds of physicians in the U.S. actively use OpenEvidence as a medical chatbot, achieving this scale in just three years compared to the 40 years it took Epic to reach under 50% physician adoption.
  • Cadence Remote Patient Monitoring: Operating in 7,000 sites of care across 100,000+ patients, Cadence integrates real-time hardware data (blood pressure, weight) with AI agents and clinical oversight. Published studies in the *New England Journal of Medicine* and *Mayo Clinic Proceedings* show Cadence reduces hospitalizations by 25%, increases blood pressure control by over 80%, drops diabetes A1C levels by nearly a full point, and saves Medicare $2.5 million every week.
  • Administrative Automation: AI is driving massive efficiency in the healthcare "back office" (revenue cycle management and coding) and "front office" (voice agents for scheduling and outreach), while scribes and decision-support tools transform the "in-office" experience.

OpenAI and the Consumer Healthcare Revolution

  • OpenAI as a Provider: OpenAI effectively acts as one of the largest healthcare providers in America, with 300 million people asking ChatGPT healthcare-related questions every week.
  • Diagnostic Benchmarking: Reasoning models (like OpenAI's o1) achieve a 10-out-of-10 success rate on House M.D.-level New England Journal of Medicine case studies—vastly outperforming human physicians who average 2 out of 10. In collaboration with Boston Children's and Harvard hospitals, AI successfully identified new diagnoses for roughly 20 out of 300 unsolved pediatric cases.

Notable Quotes

"As a nation, I think we need a 10x nurse a lot more than we need a 10x engineer." — Dr. Nworah Ayogu
"The last 10 years, we've spent about $40 trillion on healthcare. We have not moved our life expectancy an inch... If that is not an indictment of the failure of the healthcare system, I don't know what is." — Dr. Nworah Ayogu
"We don't make it to space. We don't invent silicon chips without our brain. That is the fundamental substrate that has unlocked all of this... The brain is still a more efficient engine of compute than every chip that Nvidia has ever made." — Dr. Nworah Ayogu

So yeah, so if we if we think of, you know, AI for drug discovery and where people often think of companies like Isomorphic, companies like Chai, you know, the the magic of it is saying, look, a lot of times you have a target. So you have some, you know, molecule that's out there that when we hit this target, it changes something within the body, but oftentimes we both need to understand what are the right targets to hit, right? Folks will call that, you know, biological risk. So if we do something to this target, if we change it, what happens to the rest of the body? If we upregulate it, if we downregulate it, you know, does it make you, you know, sleepier? Does it make you more awake? Does it make you lose weight? So, we need to understand what happens with that target. And then we need to understand all of the off-target effects. So, what are the side effects that it happens? So, we want to do something. We want to make you lose weight, but we also don't want to make you nauseous. We don't want to make your heart rate go up. We don't want to give you liver toxicity. Those are kind of the off-target effects. So, there's that kind of biological risk is the thing we're trying to solve. We want to do the thing we want to do and we want to make sure we don't do the things that we don't want to do. Now the flip side of that is that we then have to make a molecule to bind that target. And when you talk of all these models that are protein folding models, you know, molecular design, kind of AI for drug discovery, people often think of that problem of how do we make a how do we actually make this molecule? And some molecules are easier to make than others. You hear this term undruggable. But undruggable means that you know the pocket's small or sometimes there are molecules where, you know, I have to bind for part of it because all these things are three-dimensional shapes. So if I bind part of it, right, the molecule itself may move some so something else can bind. So they'll talk about kind of hidden pockets and things that make this stuff hard to do because you have to be able to predict in real time what it is now and how the shape changes over time. So those are kind of the things that you're often trying to predict. So with these drug discovery models, the idea is, okay, can we first create a model that will let us understand what is the best thing to make to bind this? And that's kind of step one. The next step is that, okay, I've made something that can bind here. Can I also get something that will predict what happens after I bind this thing? And then after that you also want to be able to predict, okay, but can I also predict what are the unwanted effects that I can get? And those are that's kind of the progression of the problems that we're trying to solve with these models. And I think the first step of what molecule binds well is the thing we've actually gotten, you know, better and better and better at doing. But what folks have realized is, okay, now that we're able to do that better, there's still sort of room to grow and room to improve. But the actual thing that we want to be able to solve next is being able to predict what those effects are. And you know, with with coding, with math, you know, those things are very verifiable. So I can I know whether my code compiles or not, right? For these things, I have to also verify did my molecule work. So you know, does it bind to the other? I can actually test how well does this thing bind for does this have the effect that I want that's a more complicated question to ask. So I then have to be able to you know put it into a cell and see does this change the cell in the way that I want to or you to figure out what side effects does this have in a person I need to first put it into a mouse to figure out does it do what I want it to do in a mouse. And there's there's a saying we always have in research where it's, you know, we've cured cancer in mice 50 times. So everyone's like, look, you know, if you're if you want to cure your cancer, the best thing to do is to be a mouse, right? Because we've cured that 50 million times and we have not cured it in all cases in humans. So oftentimes we'll put it in an animal and say, okay, how does it work in the animal because that's a good, you know, sometimes that's a proxy for the human, but the real best way to know how is it going to affect you as a person is to put it into another person. So those are kind of the levels of data that we need to figure out how do we test it in animals and humans more rapidly. Other people are looking at how do we actually get data that will give us good proxies for that and that's what we actually want the models to be able to predict. But before the models can predict how it will what effect it'll have, we have to have enough data to train them to make those predictions. And that's a bit of what you're hearing folks say when they say we need to generate the data to let us do that. There's both data to let us verify the experiments we're doing, but there's now another vertical of what's the data that will get the models good enough to actually make those predictions. So the idea is you take the cancer out and you sequence it and you say, "Okay, what are the parts of this tumor and this tumor's expression, its tumor's own genome that makes it unique and we're going to create a vaccine, an mRNA vaccine to target that exact cancer." So what they say is, okay, we take the cancer out, we're then going to make this vaccine that we're going to inject into you and then we're also going to give you Keytruda to kind of give you an immune boost at the same time to make it easier for your immune system to really recognize that cancer. So then any microscopic pieces of that cancer that are still kind of floating around in your body, your immune system is now boosted to just wipe all of those out. But it also because your immune system has memory. So now if that cancer happens to come back in 3 years, 4 years, your immune system will remember it and your immune system will be able to fight that cancer while the cancer is still small and sort of get rid of it. And that is that was the thesis of cancer vaccines and that's exactly how it played out. I think in their in their phase three clinical trial, they found that people who had that were 50% less likely to have a recurrence of that cancer in five years. So I think that's an example of both the frontier and the types of new treatments we're able to create, but also the fact that a key portion of these new treatments is now Keytruda, this immune checkpoint inhibitor. So I think we're going to see more growth because we're seeing because the frontier is able to tackle kind of more and more problems. Trust Test for Agentic AI

World Economic Forum(1.1M)·57:02·2.7K views·2026-10-14
Yalda AoukarSpeaker, World Economic Forum Lubna BouzaSpeaker, World Economic Forum Omar Sultan Al OlamaMinister of State for Artificial Intelligence, UAE
TL;DRAs agentic AI shifts from answering questions to executing autonomous decisions, a lack of mature governance and global consensus poses severe risks to human livelihood, safety, and trust.
Addressing this requires building strict safeguards, prioritizing human quality of life...

TL;DR

As agentic AI shifts from answering questions to executing autonomous decisions, a lack of mature governance and global consensus poses severe risks to human livelihood, safety, and trust. Addressing this requires building strict safeguards, prioritizing human quality of life over raw productivity, and moving from a paradigm of fear to active public education and alignment.

The Trust Deficit and Human Agency

  • The Currency of Trust:
    • Trust in AI cannot be decoupled from trust in human institutions; governments and corporations must actively earn the public's confidence.
    • His Highness Sheikh Mohammed bin Rashid Al Maktoum’s Insight: The true currency of the future and of governments is not energy, water, or AI, but trust. Losing it happens instantly through catastrophic events, while gaining it requires consistent, correct decisions over time.
  • Domain-Specific Tolerances:
    • Public trust depends heavily on the stakes involved. Citizens accept autonomous agents for low-risk, proactive public services (e.g., automatic passport renewals where a day's delay is tolerable), but reject them for high-stakes decisions impacting life opportunities and livelihoods.
    • Yalda Aoukar’s Perspective: Comparing agentic adoption to investment management, users shouldn't blindly delegate high-level authority to an AI agent a minute after downloading it, just as an investor wouldn't hand capital to a fund manager on day one.

Governance, Guardrails, and Corporate Accountability

  • Corporate Incentives vs. Public Harm:
    • Major AI labs (Anthropic, OpenAI, etc.) operate as profit-driven enterprises answering to shareholders, yet are often treated like non-profit research labs. Governments must hold them legally and financially accountable for negative societal impacts.
  • Design-Level Safeguards:
    • Stop-Loss Mechanisms: Omar Sultan Al Olama and Mark-Alexandre Doumba emphasize building hard technological stop-losses into agent code to prevent systems from cascading into catastrophic failures (analogous to stock market stop-losses).
    • Permission Structures: Traditional institutional tools like maker-checker workflows and tiered transaction limits must be transposed directly into multi-agent systems to ensure human oversight.
  • The Global Governance Deadlock:
    • Despite early warnings dating back to GPT-2, international bodies (UN summits in Geneva, Shanghai coalitions) have failed to forge a binding global consensus on red lines—such as banning autonomous weapon systems—largely due to geopolitical competition between major powers (US, China, Russia) where unilateral disarmament creates a strategic disadvantage.

Infrastructure, Open Models, and Investment Shifts

  • The Open-Weight Shift:
    • Token Consumption Pivot: Omar Sultan Al Olama notes a massive 13-week shift where token consumption flipped from ~80% closed-weight models (OpenAI, Anthropic) to ~80% open-weight models (notably from China) as users demand customizability and control over their own infrastructure.
  • Security as an Investable Asset:
    • Yalda Aoukar’s Investment Strategy: Bracket Capital views agentic safeguards not as optional add-ons, but as core investable infrastructure. This spans cybersecurity, fraud prevention, identity verification, and biometrics to cryptographically verify that AI agents possess the explicit authority to act on a user's behalf.
    • *Hugging Face Incident:* Swarms of self-organized AI agents successfully hacking demands illustrate the urgent need for machine-speed defense mechanisms.

Red Lines and Protecting Vulnerable Populations

  • Child Safety and Digital Harm:
    • Mark-Alexandre Doumba’s Findings: Research from their foundation on online child safety shows that AI companions and chatbots hack childhood attachment styles, fostering social isolation and synthetic relationships.
    • Just as toy manufacturers face immediate business termination for harming children, AI platforms must be held to "safety by design" and "safety by default" standards to prevent toxic psychological impacts.
  • Definitive Red Lines:
    • The UAE's Framework: Omar Sultan Al Olama states that any action affecting human livelihood is an absolute red line. The UAE mapped federal services and found a 70% tolerance threshold for AI deployment, while categorizing the remaining 30% as off-limits due to excessive risk.
    • Agent Control Tower: The UAE federal government utilizes a centralized control tower where every agent's thoughts and actions are logged, audited, and continuously assessed against quality of life improvements rather than mere productivity or monetary gains.

Public Perception, Education, and Workforce Integration

  • Overcoming Fear of the Unknown:
    • Public backlash (such as university commencement walkouts or protests over data center resource usage) stems from a lack of understanding and polarization.
    • Governments must proactively educate citizens rather than remaining passive. The UAE launched nation-wide initiatives aimed at training citizens to become "super prompters" and mandates that 100% of federal government employees complete advanced AI training by August of the following year.
  • Shifting from Displacement to Creation:
    • While entry-level jobs face downward pressure and automation anxiety, proactive skilling can turn AI from a vector of job loss into a democratizing tool for entrepreneurship—allowing non-technical individuals (e.g., media reporters) to instantly build software, launch companies, or access world-class tutoring in the Global South.

Notable Quotes

"The currency of the future is not energy, it's not AI, it's not water, it's actually trust." — Sheikh Mohammed bin Rashid Al Maktoum (cited by Yalda Aoukar)
"To you it looks like a tail light. To me, I just realized I lost a wife, a trailer, and five kids... what we need to do right now is understand what guards need to be put in place to ensure that we don't come to a moment where someone tells us 'oh, your tail lights are out' and we realize we lost all the trust that we built over the years." — Omar Sultan Al Olama
"If you have productivity gains, but you're harming people, it doesn't matter. Or economic gains, but you're harming people, doesn't matter. The direction that we got from our prime minister is we need to look at quality of life improvement." — Omar Sultan Al Olama
"Children could potentially be the ultimate stress test for agentic artificial intelligence because if we can develop tools that preserve human agency as opposed to replacing it, then I think it's win-win for all." — Yalda Aoukar
is the minister of state of artificial intelligence of the United Arab Emirates. His Excellency Mark Dumba, the minister of digital economy, digitalization and innovation of Gabon. Here the margin of error is so low and the risk on you is just for example getting your passport a day late or a day early. And as a result, because everybody recognizes that there's a lot of competitive pressures among big blocks, big countries to want to control this technology. And it is normal that everyone so long as people are seeing this from a competitive perspective we're not going to get consensus on the governance of AI. And you can have AI for the economy, you can have AI for healthcare, you can have AI for military reasons and we can have two approaches: one where we either try to agree on the path of least resistance, so on topics related to AI on fronts where we should normally agree, but we're not, and this is what His Excellency is saying when it comes to harmful AI. We should agree that this should not be something that we let run loose. But then there are topics where we could agree that AI should be a tool of productivity enhancements where the world has been struggling with productivity for the past decades or so. So and there I think we all agree that AI should be there. So I think that again, AI is a tool and it reflects the world that we live in. Trust is at its lowest point in history since we've been measuring trust in institutions. So even saying should we trust AI is should we trust the institutions? How can we first make sure that the institutions that people have gotten used to engaging with, they start trusting before they can trust AI? Because AI is just a delegation of trust from an institution to a tool that most people still don't understand. So I think there's a lot of work government needs to do to first explain why do we want agentic AI? Because we're pushing this on people without really letting them know why we want this. Government may want it for some reasons. A citizen may want it for other reasons. They are not mutually exclusive, but they also are not something where we've consulted people to say that's what you want. We believe that we should provide it to people because we can make delivery services better, business processes better. But is it what people want? And if people want it then they will learn to trust it and I think it will help us generate consensus on the way we can govern it. But we need to make progress and it's not been great, but we're moving forward I think and I think it's going to... At the national level, sir, you know we always hear that human is in the loop and I think this has been always presented as a solution. But sometimes you cannot understand the reasoning of the action of an agent or review the action in speed. And I think that would probably raise a very important question: is this a genuine oversight by the government or is it simply a rubber stamp? Well, I think it needs to be a rubber stamp because the problem is that agentic AI moves so quickly that there needs to be a built into the design of the systems ways for human intervention and for the government to be able to say, "Okay wait, this is moving quickly, that's not what I want," or if it happens I need to be able to backtrack it. And this is where you need to have permissions as to what AI agents are able to do with authorities. Current software already does that. So already if a bank is a very good example of an institution where you know you have different levels of permission in the system, operations transactions up to a certain level allowed, up to another level you have maker and checker. So these are things that we already know how to do. We just need to transpose that into AI systems, which obviously are more advanced than traditional AI assistants, but that require the same level of oversight. Because at the end of the day, if something is harmful to a citizen, the responsible entity is not the AI agent, it's the institution that has deployed the AI agent. So accountability will always be tied to a human at the end of the day. And this is I think one of the biggest challenges we'll have to determine, which is what legal statute and how we assign responsibilities across agents, across institutions, across the vendors as His Excellency mentioned. And trust, as she rightly put it, is something that we need to work on, but not just at telling people "trust agents," trust the institutions that deploy the agents. Thank you, Your Excellency. What is the one thing you will never trust the agents on? Children? Exactly. Please tell me more. Yeah, it's interesting, at our foundation we do a lot of work on online child safety and working with children in the digital world has really changed the way that I think about AI and trust. We just published our fourth flagship report that looks at AI companions and AI chatbots and the threats to childhood and how effectively these technologies are hacking the attachment styles of our children and making them more addictive to social isolation. When you're constantly interacting with an AI chatbot or an AI companion and you're mistaking that relationship, the synthetic relationship, for a real relationship, that is really very, very alarming. It's interesting, we did the same mistake with social media. We unleashed a very powerful technology on a new generation. It led to social isolation. It led to very severe repercussions. And the research shows, again as it relates to children's behavior with AI, that you need a human connection for yourself, for your children, to develop key milestones of development, of growth and development in their lives, both emotionally but also physically. And it feels like we're very much repeating now the mistake that we did with social media. Social media did not hate kids. It did not hate teenagers. It was designed in a way to maximize engagement, and engagement and well-being are not always necessarily aligned. What's interesting now with agentic AI and with AI generally is you now have these sources where children can interact, but also go to these platforms for advice as opposed to going to a trusted adult, and that inherently is a defect in how these AI structures are being designed. So something that we really advocate for is safety by design and safety by default solutions. If you think about it, you're a toy manufacturer and you put something out on the market that harms children, you're probably going to be out of business the next day. If you're a publisher and you publish toxicity on your platform, you have very severe legal ramifications around that. But that has not been applying to all these platform providers that have really developed things that are inherently very, very toxic for kids. Something that we really advocate for, and I think it goes back to what both Excellencies were saying earlier, and something just to reflect on a point that you said, something that really amazes me is how is it that there's not an international legal framework with a code of ethics that you can just program into these agents? We speak of agents as if they're real humans; they're technologies, they're tools. There must be a way to just program the safeguards within their actions. And I think once we have the legal ramifications and the financial ramifications for companies that fail to abide, we're definitely moving in the right direction in terms of providing an AI agentic system that we can trust and use and will not be used in nefarious ways. Thank you, Your Excellency. Where is the red line here? Can I maybe share an experience, the UAE's experience on this, and then maybe we'll talk about red lines at the very end. So we've talked about a lot of things relating to the bad side of AI. There's also a good side of AI and there's a good way to actually deploy AI. So as a country we've had a very aggressive set of targets that have been put in place by His Highness the President and His Highness the Prime Minister, which is 50% of government services need to be delivered by agents in the next two years. That's on the federal level. We're moving aggressively towards that. Now in light of everything that we just said previous to this, how do we stand as a country and how are we able to do that? One thing that we looked at is we need to ensure that every single agent that's being deployed is being deployed by a responsible player that understands the ramifications. So we're not just going out there and buying software from companies that don't understand the ramifications of what their agents are able to do through government services in the UAE in a way that harms people. The second thing is we're also looking at what services can cause the least harm if the decisions that are taken on these services are taken, for example, in the wrong way by an agent, right? So as I mentioned, renewing your passport, there is very little that can go wrong here. And certain things relating to renewal of documents is very easy for you to identify. There are certain other services where the agents' actions need to be held at a higher standard and we're not actually identifying that at this point of time. And that's why we said 50 because we mapped out all government services and we realized that around 70% of them there is some sort of tolerance to deploying AI in them. 30% the risk of making a mistake is actually higher than our tolerance, so we're not even looking at them. The second thing is, and maybe this was not publicly said before so this could be a first here on the stage, we have an agent control tower on the UAE federal government side that every agent needs to plug into and every action and every specific thought that the agent thinks of or action that it takes is logged. So there is an oversight that ensures that we understand what agents are doing, how they're taking certain actions. There's a continuous audit that is being done. There's an audit that's done by agents and there's an audit that's done by humans to ensure that this doesn't go haywire in any way, shape or form. And we're constantly assessing the value. So most people look at the value as His Excellency mentioned in productivity gains, right? So how much productivity have you increased? Others in certain other geographies are looking at economic incentives, so how much savings we've had, how much money we've gotten. We're not looking at either. The direction that we got from our Prime Minister is we need to look at quality of life improvement. Going back to the trust topic that was mentioned, if you're able to improve people's quality of life by deploying that agent, then you're moving in the right direction. If you have productivity gains, but you're harming people, it doesn't matter. Or economic gains, but you're harming people, it doesn't matter. So that's how we're applying it. You're going to ask me about red lines. To us, if human livelihood is going to be affected by the agent then it's a red line, especially at this point, because we don't know the true capabilities of technology and we don't think that human life or human livelihood is something that has a dollar figure value to it, it's invaluable. So we need to ensure that we are responsible as a government and that we continue to gain people's trust by ensuring that we are responsible deployers of this technology. Can I please clap? Because this is amazing honestly. This is what AI should be deployed for, the quality of life for people. That's amazing. What's the red line for you, sir? I think, I mean honestly I would not innovate on the answer because I think he's answered so well. But it just cannot harm people. It needs to be something that adds value to people's life. It needs to be something that makes a worker's day easier, allows a worker to focus on tasks that are more value-adding, that are more creative, and that removes the burden of some task. And that's on the worker, on individuals, on citizens, it's really about how can I have access to a service from my phone, from my couch, from whatever. I ask for something and sometimes, you know, we have bounded rationality, so sometimes I want something, but I don't know that I also want X, Y and Z. And this is where agentic AI can support and say, "Hey, you just had a baby. Okay, great. Well, here is the birth certificate and also insurance here and also do this and do that." And actually, this already exists in the UAE, by the way. It already exists in the UAE. Okay, that's probably why I'm talking about it. But you know, agentic AI helps you anticipate and it makes your life easier, and so long as it serves that purpose I think it's fine if AI agents can also be used and be designed to protect us from threats that we don't know of. This is also a great way to use AI. So, as far as red lines, protecting human sanity, I loved your point about using social media as an illustration to how a tool that was supposed to make our lives better has actually turned against us and now we're regulating saying that kids, children under the age of 16 should no longer be having access to—I mean, some countries are regulating that way, not all of them. But that's to say that sometimes you unleash a technology and it takes experience to then backpedal and say, "Okay, this is good, but you know what? I'm going to carve this out now because the mental health effect, which is the thing that we need to safeguard the most, are being affected." And that's where I think the red line would be. Thank you, Your Excellency Yela. Yes. And I think, you know, children, thank you for bringing that up, could potentially be the ultimate stress test for agentic artificial intelligence because if we can develop tools that preserve human agency as opposed to replacing it, then I think it's win-win for all. Thank you, Yela. So, some takeaways before I open the floor for questions: The goal is not simply to build AI that can act, but AI we can trust to act. AI should improve the quality of life of our citizens, not harm them. Let's open the floor for questions. If you have a question, please raise your hand. We will pass you the mic. Please introduce yourself and who you're addressing your question to. Okay, please, we have a question here. Hello, I'm Adam Warbach from San Francisco. Your Excellency, I've been quite frightened actually by the recent response of the American public to the growth of AI in the country and with the midterm elections coming up. This is really a kind of a key loadstone around the necks of candidates and how they think about it. Right now the average American doesn't want any more data centers built and actually would rather have a nuclear power plant near their home than a data center. To me this is actually quite frightening and the path to data centers is also the path to energy transformation, intelligence, economic growth, etc. And I'm curious what lessons you might have from here that you might share about how to develop a population that is ambitious and excited about the growth of AI rather than scared and trying to relent as we kind of build a campaign to bring the American public back in. That's a great question. Thank you. So the first thing I'm going to say relating to this is why would people be scared? You're scared of something that you don't understand or something that you don't know. There's a fear of the unknown. And the way that we dealt with this with artificial intelligence specifically and with setting the narrative for AI is once we started, the first thing we wanted to do is tackle people's ignorance. Get them to understand what AI is, what it isn't, get them to actually use it to understand what it's capable of, and get them to understand what the ramifications of not being part of that future is for our public. I'll give you a simple example. We're in October right now. The 29th of October of every year coincides with UAE Coding Day. And the last couple of years, it's become UAE AI Day. And on this day, every single individual in the country gets an SMS on their phones. And that SMS is, "You can start your journey learning what AI can do for you by the click of a button." And you click a button, it gives you a generic prompt that you can put in an AI model and then you can build a game, you can create a productivity-improving tool, you can do so much with just one prompt. And what we've realized is once people understand that, okay, this is something I can use. This is transformational. Oh wow, what would the future look like? They tend to be more optimistic. As a government, we can't afford to be pessimistic or optimistic when it comes to the technology. We need to be in the middle. We need to be dead center. We could be cautiously optimistic at times, but most times we need to be in the middle to ensure that we don't sway one way or the other. But the public needs to be optimistic because the public stands to gain a lot from this technology. You talk about data centers. The rhetoric in the US is every data center takes X gallons of water from the grid. There's another consensus there that electricity prices go up when there are data centers being put up. In the UAE, what we show people is every single data center that goes up doesn't affect the price of energy, not because it's subsidized, but because we're using solar power, but because we have nuclear power, we invested early on. The second thing is, you know, as a country we know that there's no extra water being taken from the grid because we don't have any natural water in the ground anyway, right? We need to desalinate. And at the same time we need to be effective in really creating state-of-the-art data centers that are sustainable. And then finally, most people are moving into these domains where they are becoming part of the future. The challenge in the US, and this is scary not just for the US, I think for the whole world, when you see a commencement speaker at a university just mention AI and people are booing or they walk away, it is insane. It just shows that it's so polarizing as a subject and this technology stands to change the lives of those people that are walking out of the room like we haven't done a good job educating them on the pros in a way that makes them feel like yes we are optimistic about their future. So I think there's a lot of public discourse that needs to happen where people need to understand and need to be able to have conversations and the stakeholders need to be open about it. Today you're either shunning the question or you're not even addressing it all together. The second is not every state is equal. The same as in the UAE, where a federation of seven states, not every state is equal. Some states, for example, need to build more electric infrastructure for the rural areas. Others have an excess of energy, right? And you need to make sure that people understand that, okay, I'm not going to take away the electricity that's going to come to your house to put it in a data center. That's how we're approaching it in the UAE. We want to make sure that people understand there's an excess of energy and that excess will go to a data center because today it's available capacity that's not being utilized. In the US, I think some people worry that if I'm not getting the electricity and if I'm overpaying for it, why are you giving it to the data center? And third, they're saying, "Okay, how does that affect me? The data center is not something I'm going to utilize. It's not a job that's going to be created for me." And giving them that angle is going to be important. I think it's extremely dangerous to be in the point where you tell people not to build. One issue that I see is imagine a hundred years ago when they came to build the electricity power plants, those who were selling wood fire, you know, wood and coal went and started polarizing people against electric production stations being put up. If the US did not electrify, it would have been a disaster. Electrifying today and digitizing today and giving people intelligence at the tip of their fingertips because of data centers is necessary. I think it's a very dangerous thing that needs to be tackled. Thank you, Your Excellency. Do we have any—okay, one more question before we wrap up because we're out of time. I think we have so many questions, just to build on this point. I think there's a big concern with regards to jobs. I think that the narrative about the impact of AI, even without talking about agentic AI, is that it will replace workers, it will displace jobs. I think this is the most threatening narrative that you can give to a population in places where unemployment rates have been rising over time. Is it because of automation? Most likely, it's one of the big factors of it. So then suddenly you're saying that AI agents that have already perceptibly impacted the job market will now be amplified to agentic AI where agents will be telling other agents what to do and ultimately it should be reducing your work time, but increasingly it looks like many companies are just not hiring, especially at entry-level jobs. We're seeing this crisis in the education system. The US is very—obviously it's a big topic there—where people graduating with a bachelor's degree or even from grad school are not able to find entry-level jobs because most of those early-level tasks are being great at being automated through AI and agentic AI. And you have—I think there was this graduation speech by one of the big tech CEOs where he was booed by the student class because they were basically upset that because of those progresses in technologies, jobs at the entry level are not there. But if you cannot have an entry-level job how can you have a later entry job? You just can't. So I think we need to be able to say that AI for white-collar workers is different from AI for somebody who's working on the construction site. It means different things for different people. So the message needs to reflect that. You need to be able to address your target audience accordingly in order to preempt the negative perception that comes with agentic AI. Your Excellency, do you really believe that the loss of jobs because of AI will be unprecedented? And do you really think we should call for probably to reserve some occupations in particular for humans in important sectors like I don't know health, education? Probably do you think we will get there one day? I'm just going to build on His Excellency's point, how we tackled job loss and this perception is we went on a very audacious and ambitious program to actually get everyone to become a super prompter of AI in the country. So we've done two initiatives: one, which is actually going to be concluded this month, to train a million people how to become super prompters of AI. So regardless of their background, they could be a janitor, they could be a CEO, they could be a student, it's free for anyone to embark on this program, but they learn how to get the most out of these systems. So they get much more from them than an individual that is not trained. The second thing is there's also a fear of job loss within government. So actually we have a target by August of next year every single employee in the government regardless of their level needs to also go through an extensive AI training that's making them super users of AI. We've already trained half of the employees in the federal government and we're training the remaining 50%. But what happens is when people are super users of these tools, you give them two possible futures: one, to do their jobs much better; second is they're able to unlock possible futures that they couldn't unlock in the past, which is launch their own company, create new domains or new products, understand that there are certain barriers to entry that they could not really get into that is suddenly made available to them. Think about, for example, coding jobs. If I ask everyone in the room here, "Could you develop your own software?" Two years ago the answer would have been no. Today anyone can build their own software and you know I don't know if you've seen the latest announcement by Google you can now publish AI generated content on the Google Play Store there's a closed Google play store for that and you can actually get people to test it and use it and then maybe ultimately publish it for the rest of the people online. But today that barrier that existed two years ago where you know I'm a media reporter for example I'm not technical I can't go into actual coding software today you can and you can build the best software in the world because of the availability of these tools. So, we're opening people's eyes to that. We're seeing a lot of very positive outcomes because of it. We will share that for the rest of the world. I think generally speaking, the UAE's model is unique. So, I don't think you can apply that to the US for example or China that has 1.3 billion people or India. But maybe there is something here that can be taken by these countries and scaled to their level. Thank you very much, and changing the paradigm as well as we move from fear of AI for job loss to AI for job creation and job access. If you think of the global south and I'm sure Gabo is a perfect case in point, you have children in underserved community that could never have access to world class shooters and now all of a sudden AI can provide that to them. And the job market suddenly opens up to those populations. And on that very positive note, Yela, I would wrap up because I'm out of time. Actually, we we we took more time than we should. Thank you very much, dear speakers, our distinguished guests for sharing with us your experience, your thoughts. We really appreciate it. What Could Break the AI Boom? Cerebras’ Andrew Feldman Has an Idea | Disrupt 2026

TechCrunch(676.0K)·29:28·2026-10-14
Andrew FeldmanCEO, Cerebras
TL;DR- **TL;DR:** Cerebras CEO Andrew Feldman argues that AI infrastructure demand will continue to scale globally despite local community pushback and data center constraints, while hardware efficiency and giant wafer-scale architectures will remain critical to meeting compute needs.
  • TL;DR: Cerebras CEO Andrew Feldman argues that AI infrastructure demand will continue to scale globally despite local community pushback and data center constraints, while hardware efficiency and giant wafer-scale architectures will remain critical to meeting compute needs.

Data Center Expansion & Power Constraints

  • Global vs. US Sentiment: Community Pushback: Feldman noted that while the US AI industry failed to work effectively with local communities regarding power and data centers—leading to protests and public fear amplified by "maniacally focused" safety leaders—international markets (Canada, Norway, Finland, Sweden, Iceland, Mexico, Brazil) are actively inviting data center builders in.
  • Sustained Demand: Growth Drivers: AI demand continues to surge across both large players and smaller newcomers like Instacart, requiring continuous data center expansion to house increasing compute workloads.
  • Efficiency Integration: Multi-Layer Efficiency: Feldman stated that efficiency and scaling are not at odds; improvements must happen across power delivery, rack switching, hardware compute, and model-level design to maximize revenue per 20-megawatt power envelope.

Cerebras Technology & Market Competition

  • The Wafer-Scale Chip: Physical Scale: Cerebras builds single chips measuring 46,000 square millimeters (making it ~55 times larger than the previous industry max of 804–840 sq mm), holding more information and processing inference up to 750–800 tokens per second.
  • Overcoming Technical Hurdles: Development Toll: The company survived an 18-month period burning roughly $8 million per month while trying to get initial chips to survive past 8 seconds.
  • Customer Base & Scaling Pains: Enterprise Traction: Cerebras counts OpenAI, Amazon AWS, neo-clouds, Cognition, Lovable, Block, Figma, and GSK as customers, alongside US government and UAE sovereign cloud deals.
  • OpenAI Relationship: Capacity Limits: While OpenAI early investors Sam Altman and Greg Brockman made modest angel investments in Cerebras, OpenAI utilized Nvidia for GPT-1 Soul UltraFast because Cerebras lacked the necessary data center capacity at the time to support OpenAI's goal of going vastly wider.
  • Competitive Landscape: Market Diversity: Feldman dismissed the idea of a single undisputed king, noting that companies like AMD ($1 trillion valuation), Arm, Marvell (partnering with Google), and Cerebras will coexist in a massive market.

Security, Regulation, and AI Safety

  • Chip-Level Safety: Hardware Guardrails: Commenting on Nvidia's open agent safety program (Sentry), Feldman acknowledged that monitoring and shutting down rogue agents at the silicon level is wise marketing and engineering, noting that Cerebras also possesses techniques to shut down misbehaving software at the chip level.
  • Multi-Layer Control: Infrastructure-Wide Safeguards: Controlling rogue traffic and preventing misuse requires a coordinated effort across model makers, cloud providers, hardware vendors, and network-layer security built by companies like Arista, Juniper, and Cisco.
  • Legal and Regulatory Gaps: Infrastructure Deficit: Feldman stated that the legal and regulatory system has a "giant hole" and was entirely unprepared for AI innovations like training data ownership and autonomous agent risks.

Entrepreneurship and Deep Tech Survival

  • The Deep Tech Playbook: Methodology Over Fear: Feldman advised founders facing existential dread to rely on systematic failure analysis rather than quitting, summarizing: "If you're not out of money, you're not out of ideas."
  • Financial Milestones: IPO & Wealth Generation: Following a successful IPO in May raising $5.5 billion, Cerebras has generated over 1,000 employee millionaires across its history.
  • Tesla Acquisition Offer: Passed Opportunity: Feldman recalled turning down a buyout offer from Elon Musk (via Tesla) during a dinner conversation, choosing instead to remain independent.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Industry Structure: Feldman predicts that the AI chip market will feature many highly successful companies rather than a single undisputed king, citing AMD, Arm, Marvell, and Cerebras alongside traditional giants.
  • Local AI Appliances: Feldman predicts that personal AI assistants running locally on dedicated hardware with a security perimeter will soon become commonplace, much like a home appliance.

Notable Quotes

*"It's really important that the people who know least about the technology have the loudest opinions on stuff like a kill switch."* — Andrew Feldman
*"One of the great things about doing deep tech is you begin, everyone says it'll never work. And then you make it work and they say, 'Okay, it worked, but it'll never go to production. It's too hard to make.' And then we... just keep crushing them, whacking them on the head."* — Andrew Feldman
*"If you're not out of money, you're not out of ideas."* — Andrew Feldman
The theme of our next session is the fact that AI is getting bigger. Bigger models, bigger data centers, more chips, more power, and increasingly enormous amounts of money, which is possibly why you're here today. But, can that continue indefinitely? Our next guest has spent years challenging some of the fundamental assumptions about how AI compute should work. So, please welcome Cerebras co-founder and CEO Andrew Feldman in conversation with TechCrunch's Julie Bort. Hi, Andrew. How you going, Julie? Welcome to Disrupt. Thank you guys for having me. You are having a hell of a year. Big year. Just I'm going to brief through some of the stuff. So, Cerebras makes AI systems powered by its own custom giant chip, super fast inference chip. He brought one to show you. You took your company public in May in a very successful IPO, raising 5.5 billion. You have Open AI and Amazon AWS as big customers, as well as a lot of neo clouds and customers like Cognition, Lovable, Block, Figma, pharmaceutical giant GSK. But, there are some signals that the data center build out is, you know, losing support, at least from those that are financing it. So, Oracle's stock has been hurt by all of its debts. Australian AI cloud firm has canceled its IPO. Amazon wrote this impassioned plea to US citizens to support data centers. So, the question that we're here to answer, this is what I want you to answer. Can AI keep scaling like it has been in the past few years? Wow. I get to comment on Oracle stock. I think a couple things. I think the demand for AI is growing at an extraordinary rate. And you see that in the continued growth of the largest players, and you see that in the sort of mind-boggling growth of smaller players, new players, like Instacart, any number of these others who came from nothing to extraordinary numbers. Now, when more people use AI and they use it for longer, it means they're using more compute. And when you use more compute, it means you have to put it somewhere. And that somewhere is a data center. And it's not super complicated. We put them in buildings that use a great deal of power. And as an industry, we weren't super good for the past few years at working with local communities. Particularly in the US, the industry didn't do what others have done in the power industry and oil and gas. They didn't work with the community, they didn't engage the community as they should have, and that was a problem. And then on a separate note, we had some of the leaders in the AI world sort of maybe shouldn't be allowed to talk in public. I think what they're maniacally focused on dangers and negativity and not telling people that, you know, maybe your grandkids never meet anybody who dies of a cancer. And they've made our society a little afraid. And it's hard to be afraid of AI, it's hard to know where to point your fear, and so they pointed at data centers. And the irony is in Canada, in Norway, in Finland, in Sweden, and Iceland, in Mexico, in Brazil, they're encouraging people to build data centers. And the data center builders are working, including us, are working with local communities, and the communities are inviting us in. And so, I think that was a very long answer to the question that, A, it's growing like crazy, B, Well, demand is growing like Demand is growing like crazy, but so are data center build-outs. And often, when one community gets up in arms, the builders move to a different one or a different location or different country. There's also another thing that's happening in AI right now, and that is efficiency. So, we're seeing like compression models, we're seeing competitors to you like Etch, that say that they're going to be more efficient. We're seeing sort of this rise of let's just not do more, more, more. Let's use what we've got more efficiently. So, my question is like, so okay, is are you on board with that? And what needs to happen in order for us to become more efficient rather than just continuously building more data centers? I think that they're not at odds with each other. I think we need more data centers, but we also need to be more efficient with the data centers we build. And I think, where does power go? Power first is delivered to the door of the data center, and some of it's lost in moving to the rack. There are opportunities to be more efficient there. Once you're at the rack, there's opportunities to be more efficient in the switching and in the communication. Then, once you get into the compute, there are opportunities for the compute to improve. And we can do a job as hardware vendors to make our compute more efficient. And then, the model makers can do improved work in making their models more efficient to achieve the same goals. All of this has to come together to make meaningful strides forward. I mean, one of the reasons we went to a big chip is because by moving data on a piece of silicon, and do so in an extraordinarily efficient, power domain.
